# Engine Commands

*Reference for **[leeway-engine](../SKILL.md)***

---

The CLI door: `node .claude/skills/leeway-engine/scripts/engine.cjs <command> [args]`. Skill prose prescribes these calls at exact points; this catalogue exists for understanding a command's full contract.

**Response contract (every mutation/transaction):** one decision-ready JSON line on stdout — `{"ok": true, …}` with derived state riding so no follow-up read is needed. Failures: `{"ok": false, "error": "…"}` on stderr, exit 1. Knowledge-base side effects are warn-don't-block — failures land in `warnings`, never abort. Commands that commit report `committed` (short sha, or `null` plus a note on a clean scope — the code commit, `commit --paths`, a list of `{repo, sha}`, `[]` beside the note); commands documented as commit-free rely on the calling flow's commit cadence. The workflows' state is `.leeway/` in the repository's main checkout, a worktree of its own branch, `leeway-state` (`kernel/state-root.cjs`, `domain/state-worktree.cjs`): a commit there — a state commit — is made on that branch and pushed, and a commit of code or of the files the workflows own in the code tree on the branch checked out (see **`commit`**). Every command runs whole under the state's writer lock, `.leeway/.lock`, its writes and its commit one hold, so no peer's write lands between them — but those `WRITER_LOCK_EXEMPT` in `engine.cjs` names, which read, write outside the state, or take the lock around their own writes alone, and, beside them, the `manifest` reads and the code commit (`commit --paths`); the work unit's lock and the project lock the entries below name are that one lock, and a write refuses where the state worktree is not there. Every engine-made commit is confined to the paths its action wrote — a state commit with the layout's logs changed since — and none carries the knowledge directory — git-ignored in the state, the store a derived index each clone builds for itself. One noun answers otherwise, deliberately: `knowledge`, the knowledge base's door, answers in text on both streams (see its **Answers in text, not JSON**).

## Grammar

Every command follows six rules — new commands must too:

1. **Identity is positional, in containment order**: `{work-unit}`, then `{topic}`, then `{item}`.
2. **Required values with closed vocabularies are positional enums** (`topic cancel {wu} {stage} {topic}`, `cache stamp {wu} gap-analysis`, `workunit create {wu} {work-type}`).
3. **Payloads are always named flags, even when required** (`-m {message}`, `--findings-file {path}`).
4. **Optional modifiers and alternate addressing are flags** (`--skipped`, `--parent`, `--external {id}`).
5. **Mappings are `key=value` pairs** (`topic order {wu} {topic}={order} …`).
6. **A reserved word sharing a slot with user-named values must be a flag** — why `commit --inbox`/`--workflows`/`--product` are flags beside positional `commit {work-unit}`.
Two carve-outs. The **`render` surface catalogue** passes every non-address scalar as a flag (`--gate`, `--phase`, `--m`, `--variant`, …), including required closed vocabularies — the address is the only positional. Uniformity across the render surfaces beats rule 2 there. The **`knowledge`** noun takes identity as flags (`--work-unit`, `--phase`, `--topic`), against rule 1: each is a filter narrowing the chunks a verb acts on, never an address, and a query's positionals are its search terms.

**A topic the person names** is taken by its key or by the title a rename gave it (8.8.17) wherever their naming reaches — the map's verbs, `topic send`, `cancel`, `reactivate` and `postpone`, `enter`, `handoff topic`, and every render addressed at a topic phase's topic — one rule resolving either to the key (`topicKey`, `domain/derivations.cjs`). What they answer and write names the key, and the calls after an entry take the key it answered. The map's verbs and a topic's `cancel`, `reactivate` and `postpone`, with their gates and receipts, refuse a name no topic goes by naming the live topics as they go by (`no topic "{name}" — the map's topics are {names}`), or saying there are none (`… — the map has no live topics`).

**The guards** answer otherwise too. `layout guard`, `brief guard` and `shell guard` (`guards.cjs`) are Claude Code's hooks before a tool call — Write, Edit and NotebookEdit; Agent and SendMessage; Bash — which boot installs in every project's `.claude/settings.json`, each fed the call's JSON on stdin and acting from its `cwd`, else the folder the hook runs for. Each answers in Claude Code's terms: silent where the call goes through as made; the hook's answer on stdout where it hands the call on changed; a refusal on stderr with exit 2, the code Claude Code blocks the call on. Any other failure lets the call through, and a stray argument is a usage error, exit 1. A guard stands before every call of its tools, so the shell door runs it before the engine loads anything else, loading only the guard's own module, and it takes no lock. Each is under its own noun: **`layout`**, **`brief`**, **`shell`**.

## Commands

**`boot`** — the entry pipeline (`domain/boot.cjs`). **The git floor.** First it reads the git on the PATH, once: one older than the engine's floor (`GIT_FLOOR` in `domain/git-floor.cjs`, 2.40.0 — the landing replays a task's commits with `git merge-tree --merge-base`), or none, stops boot before anything else runs. **The repository.** Next, a folder in no git repository stops it, with nothing written anywhere, the config directories included: Leeway runs inside a git repository, and only there, and never sets one up. A project that is a folder inside a larger repository is in one, and boots on. Then it drops the calling conversation's position — the start menu is no position — keeping the place it left, where that names a phase, for the board until a position is recorded again (`left.json`), and appends the trust-log events any conversation keeps waiting (`trust-pending.jsonl`, see **`conversation`**), a boot that stops below included. **The state** comes next, before anything writes it (`domain/state-checks.cjs`), under a lock of its own in the main checkout's git directory, two boots checking one after the other. `.leeway/` is kept in the repository's own exclude file, which no branch carries. The remote is asked once where its `leeway-state` stands. A state branch this clone lacks is made from the remote's — a fresh clone — or, under a folder still there, at the commit the folder's files stand at, which the catch-up then moves on; one the remote lacks is pushed back to it. The worktree is added where it is gone, relinked where the project folder moved or was copied — a copy's state becoming its own, a warning naming the original, which is left untouched — and locked where it is not. With no state branch here or on the remote: a project on the layout before the state branch — `.workflows/` on the code branch checked out — stops for the migration, with nothing written; one whose repository holds the workflows' trace — the workflows' hooks in its `.claude/settings.json`, or a line naming `.leeway/` in its `.gitignore`, on any branch, the remote's fetched first, or in any tree checked out — lost its state, and boot stops until the person's yes, `boot --start-again`, which makes a fresh state; and a project with neither is being set up, and gets one. A fresh state is the orphan branch, its first commit (`chore: begin leeway-state`) built with plumbing so the main checkout's branch, HEAD and index are never touched — the state's `.gitignore` and the project manifest holding the project's `project_id`, minted there once and never changed, which keys the project's evidence in the trust log (`manifest set` refuses it) — then the worktree added and locked. Boot refuses, with nothing written to the state or the code, where the remote is out of reach and this clone holds no state branch of its own, where the remote's branches cannot be fetched to read the trace from, where a `leeway-state` holds code history, and where the state's folder stands in a shape it cannot account for — there while its branch is gone everywhere, or with another branch checked out. A remote out of reach is never read as holding nothing: boot works on what this clone holds, with a warning. **While the migration runs** — `leeway-state` checked out at `.leeway/` with no commit yet, and `.workflows/` still on the code branch checked out — boot takes the state as the migration's move left it: the worktree is locked where it is not, and nothing is made, restored or pushed; under the writer lock only the migrations run; and boot commits nothing, its session hooks written and left to the migration's commit. **Under the writer lock** (`.leeway/.lock`), held around these alone: the ledger's entries a killed write, or a commit git refused, left uncommitted are committed (`ledger: the entries a write left uncommitted`), a warning counting them — only where each is a whole line a read takes, else nothing is committed and a warning names the line; the catch-up from the remote runs (**`state-merge`**), whose stop on a manifest field both clones changed ends boot there; the session's own `.gitignore` gets the state's ignore line, `.leeway/`, the workflows' trace, committed on the branch checked out (`chore: ignore .leeway/, the workflows state`) as the session hooks are below; and the migrations run: `leeway-migrate/scripts/migrate.cjs`, resolved relative to the engine, from the code root, recording into the state's `.state/migrations` and journaling what a run owes the run that completes — its handbacks and its counts, so a run that stops at a directive or aborts loses neither — in the state's cache, `.leeway/.cache/migrate/`. **A stop** answers `{"ok": true, "state": "git"|"repo"|"lost"|"migrate"|"merge", "warnings": […]}` and nothing else — `git` drawn by `render git-floor-stop`, `repo` by `render repo-stop`, `lost` by `render state-lost-gate`, its yes's next `boot --start-again`, `merge` by `render state-merge-gate`, each row's next its `state-merge pick`; `migrate` has no screen, the reshape's migration being the way on. **A directive** the migrations record does not hold stops the run (the format and the runner's stop are the header of `leeway-migrate/scripts/migrate.cjs`), and boot goes no further, committing nothing: it answers `{"ok": true, "state": "directive", "id", "file", "done", "of", "warnings": […]}` — the directive's id, `NNN-{slug}`, and its file, and its pieces done of all of them as its check counts them (**`migrate`**) — both left out where the check fails, as it may on what a session that died part-way left, and `migrate check` names why. `render directive-stop --directive <id>` draws the stop; the session carries the directive out and closes its checklist, which records it (**`checklist`**), then boots again — one start may meet several. Past the stop points, boot renders the ledger's copies under `.knowledge/` again (a failure a warning), then runs `knowledge check`, then — when ready — brings the store's keyword side in line with the files (the bulk `knowledge index` without its wait for vectors) and compacts it, launching the background `knowledge fill` for the vectors the store awaits. Where a decision landed unchecked and no late check has judged it, boot launches the late one-decision check, detached (**`ledger`**); what keeps it from launching is a warning. Response: `{"ok": true, "state": "ready"|"restarted", "migrations": {"changed": false, "ran": 0, "output": "<trimmed report>", "verify": [], "notices": []}, "knowledge": "ready"|"not-ready", "indexed": false, "compacted": false, "migrations_committed": null, "warnings": [], "tmux_labels": "no-tmux"|"on"|"off"|"prompt", "label_repaired": false, "session_hooks_installed": false, "gate_surface": "on"|"not-running"|"outdated"|"unavailable", "brownfield_offer": "none"|"native"|"skipped"|"accepted"|"completed", "walkthrough": "none"|"walked"|"skipped", "github_rule_offer": false, "github_squash_offer": false}`. `state` is `restarted` where this boot started a lost state again on the person's yes, else `ready`. `github_rule_offer` is true where the state's remote is on GitHub, the code host's driver can set the rule, and the offer of a rule there against deleting or force-pushing `leeway-state` was never answered — `render github-rule-gate` offers it, and **`github-rule`** answers; `github_squash_offer` where the project lands through GitHub and the squash default was never answered — `render github-squash-gate`, answered by **`github-squash`**. The knowledge directory `.leeway/.knowledge/` — the store, its metadata, the knowledge config, beside them the store's writer lock (`.lock`) and the vector fill's claim (`.fill`) — sits in the one state every checkout and worktree of the project shares, git-ignored there and never committed. `tmux_labels` reports the session-label opt-in read from the project manifest's `defaults.tmux_labels` (see `session`): `prompt` means the session runs inside tmux and the choice was never recorded — leeway's one-time prompt keys on it; every other value renders nothing. `label_repaired` reports the `session repair` pass boot runs — true when a label on this terminal was put back to its original name: the calling session's own (leeway is the start menu), or a stranded one whose owner is gone; informational, nothing renders on it. `session_hooks_installed` reports boot's sync of the session hooks in the project's `.claude/settings.json` — SessionEnd's `presence cleanup` and `conversation end`, and PreToolUse's `layout guard` (matcher `Write|Edit|NotebookEdit`), `brief guard` (matcher `Agent|SendMessage`) and `shell guard` (matcher `Bash`), each its own group, for every project, SessionEnd's `session cleanup` and SessionStart's `session resume` while labels are on — true only when this boot changed the file; false when it already carried exactly those. Neither the hooks nor the state's ignore line above is written while git holds a merge, cherry-pick, revert, rebase or bisect open in the code tree — a later boot writes them. Boot commits its own change to either file confined to it, on the branch checked out (`chore: install workflow session hooks`), and only where that change is the file's whole difference from the branch: a file that also holds the person's own uncommitted changes is theirs to commit, a warning saying so, and the next boot commits the workflows' change once theirs is in; a file the project's git ignores is never committed; a commit git refuses is a warning, and the next boot tries again. When `migrations.changed` is true the hooks' commit is left to the reviewed `commit --migrations`, which takes every path the workflows own. Informational, nothing renders on it. Boot also tidies the conversation folders (see `conversation`) — every one, whichever project's conversation made it: a folder whose `transcript` names a file that no longer exists is deleted, unless trust-log events still wait in it, and nothing is reported. `gate_surface` reports the `leeway-gates` mod, part of the workflows. It runs only in Claude Code's terminal app or the Desktop app's Code tab (`CLAUDE_CODE_ENTRYPOINT` is `cli`, `claude-desktop` or `claude-desktop-3p`; not on the web, where `CLAUDE_CODE_REMOTE` is set), in a project that installed it (`.claude/skills/leeway-gates/`), from version 2.1.287 (read off `AI_AGENT`, `claude-code_2-1-287_agent`), where Claude Code loads mods by default. Boot reads and writes no settings file for it. Outside those two apps — the VS Code extension, a Desktop session that runs in the cloud (`remote_desktop`), any other entrypoint — in a project without the mod, or where `AI_AGENT` is absent or does not read as a version, it reports `unavailable`, and the workflows carry on with the text menus; at a readable release before 2.1.287 it reports `outdated`. Where the mod can run, `on` means it is running: its announcement, `LEEWAY_GATE_SURFACE=1`, is in boot's own environment; `not-running` means it is not — the session started before the mod was installed or updated, mods are turned off (`--safe-mode`, `--bare`, `"disableAllHooks": true`, or an organization's policy), or Anthropic's rollout switch serves installed mods off. leeway's Step 0.2 shows a notice on `not-running` and carries on with typed menus, and ends the session on `outdated`; `on` and `unavailable` render nothing. `brownfield_offer` reports the answer to the brownfield offer from the project manifest (`brownfield_offer`) — `none` means nothing recorded yet (a missing manifest or unrecognised value also reads `none`), the state leeway's one-time Step 0.6 judgment keys on: a project that grew up on the workflows records `native` and is never asked again; a codebase that predates them gets the offer, whose yes records `accepted` (its research owed, which the product's conversation resumes at its next opening until it lands as `completed`; the start menu's `p` row notes it meanwhile) and whose no records `skipped` (**`brownfield`**). A `none` response additionally carries `"baseline_signal"` — the project's own account of what came before the workflows, read across every repo in it (the repos `code` reads), handed to that judgment as evidence to read: `{"root_date", "workflows_date", "commits_total", "commits_before", "history_before", "files_at_arrival", "tree_at_arrival"}` — the author date of the earliest first commit in any repo; the author date of the state's first commit, the workflows' arrival (null when the state holds none yet, so the whole history predates the workflows); every commit reachable from each repo's HEAD, summed — a project inside a larger repository reading the commits that touched its folder, and a shallow nested clone adding its tree alone; the commits before the arrival, summed — in each repo the last commit on its first-parent line made before the state's first commit, and that commit's ancestors — and those commits as `date  subject`, oldest first across the repos (head and tail kept around one elision line when the run is long); the project files in the tree the workflows arrived into, named from the project — each repo's at that last commit, a submodule's pointer never one of them (outside `.claude/`; each repo's HEAD tree when the state holds no commit yet) — and that tree's top-level shape (`dir/ (n)` and root files, largest first, elided past a dozen); a repo with no commits yet adds nothing — or `null` when there is no honest history to read (no repository, no commits in any repo, a shallow root, a failed read). Every read is bounded and tolerant: the signal is advisory and never makes boot unusable. `walkthrough` reports the answer to the walkthrough offer from the project manifest (`walkthrough.status`) — `none` means nothing recorded yet (a missing manifest or unrecognised value also reads `none`), the state leeway's one-time offer keys on; the two project-level records are independent, and neither says anything about the other. `migrations.verify` carries the verification addenda of migrations executed this boot — `{id, description, info, verify}` per entry, where `info` is what the migration does (project-agnostic) and `verify` what to check in this project; the calling skill performs those checks with judgment before its migration confirm gate and folds fixes into the migration commit. Addenda never re-fire for recorded migrations; an unreadable addenda block degrades to a warning. `migrations.notices` carries what migrations executed this boot hand back for the person — `{id, description, notice}` per entry, `notice` a sentence the calling skill shows in its migration summary; like the addenda, never re-fired, an unreadable block a warning. `migrations.changed` mirrors the orchestrator's own files-updated signal; `migrations.ran` counts the migrations executed, which is the other fact — each over the runs since the last that completed — a migration that ran and found nothing to do still recorded its ID, so a run can leave the migrations record, `.state/migrations`, dirty under a report of no changes. Whenever `changed` is false boot commits a dirty record itself, a state commit confined to it (`chore: record workflow migrations`, sha in `migrations_committed`, null when there was nothing to commit, and a commit failure a warning, never a block: the record is already right on disk). That dirt is the line this run recorded or one an earlier boot left: a boot that runs no migrations at all commits it too. When `changed` is true the calling skill's reviewed `commit --migrations` carries the record in with the diff the user approved, and boot leaves it alone. An unreadable run report degrades to a warning, `ran` reading 0. `output` is the report with the prose stop-gate lines stripped (update counts kept). A failing migrate.cjs is a hard error (`{ok:false}` on stderr, exit 1) — migrations must never half-run silently. A project that is set up — its `.leeway/.knowledge/config.json` — but has no store gets one built: `check` answers `buildable` when this machine's config says how (a provider whose key resolves, or keyword-only chosen outright in the system config or pinned by the project config), boot's keyword pass creates the store, and `check` again decides whether it stood. A failing `check`, or any other not-ready state — no project config, no system config, an invalid one, a provider whose key cannot be resolved — reports `not-ready`, and boot never sets a project up itself: a not-ready response additionally carries `"system_config": {"status": "valid"|"absent"|"invalid", "provider": <name|null>, "model": <name|null>}` — read from `~/.config/leeway/config.json` only, never the credentials file, so no secret can enter the response — which the calling skill's knowledge gate branches on to drive `knowledge setup` conversationally. `indexed` and `compacted` report that each step ran clean — `indexed` that the keyword side came in line with every file. A knowledge config setting the load ignores, a file that fails to index, a failing keyword pass or compact, a provider key that does not resolve, and a vector fill that fell short each land in `warnings` as an entry of their own (`knowledge config: …`, `knowledge index failed: …`, `knowledge compact failed: …`, `knowledge vectors wait: …`, `knowledge vector fill fell short: …`), never a block — leeway surfaces them and continues. The conversational pieces (migration summary, review gate, the knowledge gate) stay in the calling skill.

```bash
engine boot                  # every start
engine boot --start-again    # the person's yes at the lost-state stop: a fresh state where it is still lost
```

**`state-merge`** — the person's pick at the stop boot's catch-up leaves (`domain/state-merge.cjs`; the merge by path `domain/state-merge-paths.cjs`, the rules `domain/state-merge-rules.cjs`). **The catch-up** runs at every boot, before anything writes the state, losing nothing and never rewriting what was pushed: where only the remote's `leeway-state` moved the branch fast-forwards to it; where only this clone's moved it pushes what a failed push left behind; where both moved it merges. No remote, or one holding no state branch, leaves nothing to do; a remote out of reach is a warning, and the session works on the state it has. A side holding code history is refused before anything is merged or pushed. **The merge** is one new commit (`state: merge onto the remote's leeway-state`) whose parent is the remote's tip, its tree merged path by path from the common ancestor. A path one side changed takes that side's version. Where both changed it: the ledger takes the remote's lines, then this clone's the remote lacks, and the migrations record likewise; each manifest merges field by field (a list both sides only appended to keeps both sides' appends; the project manifest's `project_id` is the remote's wherever they differ); the tracker's store merges by its task driver, by the tracker's own records — a record one side changed takes that side, one both changed merges field by field, its notes and links unioned, a parent's status settled from the merged children as the tracker's own rules leave it, whoever set it, but one the person cancelled — and where a phase or task was made on both machines under one name, the remote's keeps it, this machine's is set aside with all it holds, and this machine's whole copy is kept beside the merged one; where both wrote a build's runs under the same numbers, this machine's new runs in that folder follow the remote's highest, in their own order, a note saying so; a unit's notes for later (`build/notes.md`) merge by note — the remote's stand at their numbers; one of this machine's its ancestor holds alike at its number keeps it; one the remote holds alike (the same text, held for the same phase or none) is that one, at its own number first, then anywhere; and this machine's others follow the remote's highest, in their own order, each still held for its phase, a note saying which moved — and where any of this machine's moved, each consolidation report this machine changed in that unit has the notes it takes renamed with them, every other byte as it was, and the ancestor's copy too, so a report both changed merges in one numbering, the remote's reports naming the remote's numbers, which stand; a copy of the notes that does not read refuses the merge, naming it and its side; other text goes through git's three-way merge, both versions of lines both changed kept, the remote's first; and any other file, other JSON among it, is kept whole, the remote's at the path and this machine's beside it, each such keep a note in `warnings`. Every note and refusal of the merge names its paths from the project root (`.leeway/…`). The merge is then checked as every state commit's ledger is, and pushed with a lease on the tip it was merged onto, merged again a bounded number of times where the remote moved meanwhile; this clone's unpushed commits are replaced by it. The working tree then moves to the merge's tree, its uncommitted files merged in and left uncommitted, its notes the branch's merge numbered at the numbers it gave them and any left uncommitted after the merge's, its uncommitted consolidation reports carried to the notes' numbers as the branch's are; the landing is recorded first, and a landing a kill cut short is finished before anything else. **The stop.** A field of a manifest or of the tracker's store both sides changed to different values stops the merge with nothing written, and so does a name made on both machines in the tracker's store, and so does a consolidation report of this machine's that does not read where its unit's notes moved, since what it names cannot be found — set aside, the remote's copy, or none, at its path and this machine's beside it, named by its blob's id — and so does a field the working tree's uncommitted copy and the merge changed differently, once every field on the branch is picked. The stop is kept in the state worktree's git directory, never on the branch, so it outlives the session; each choice keeps its pick, and the merge is worked out afresh from the sides each time, asking only a choice it has not met before. `render state-merge-gate` draws the one choice it asks now — the field and the two values, a task's by its name and in the engine's words; or the names made twice, what of this machine's to make again — a phase set aside by its title with its tasks, a task under a phase kept by the phase's number and title — and where its whole copy is kept, under one row that carries on; or a report set aside, how this machine's notes moved and where each copy is, under one row that carries on; a field's screen says where names made twice or reports set aside follow its step's fields — its DATA carrying the choice's identity. `pick <1|2> <choice>` answers it — `1` keeps this machine's value, `2` the remote's; at names made twice or a report set aside only `1`, carrying on — naming the choice the screen showed; a pick naming any other than the one the merge now asks is not taken (`warnings` says so, the new question in `conflicts`), and the last pick finishes the merge, its `warnings` kept beside the stop for the next catch-up, whose `warnings` — boot's — carry them once. It refuses where no merge waits on a pick, and any key but `1` or `2`. Response `{"ok": true, "action": "nothing"|"unreachable"|"pushed"|"fast-forwarded"|"merged"|"stopped", "reason": "no-remote"|"no-remote-branch"|"current"|null, "remote", "commit", "pushed", "uncommitted": {"kept": […], "merged": […]}, "conflicts": […], "warnings": […]}` — `stopped` with the choices still open, `commit` the local branch's tip after.

```bash
engine state-merge pick <1|2> <choice>   # the person's pick at the state merge's stop; <choice> from the screen's DATA
```

**`github-rule`**, **`github-squash`** — the person's answer to setup's offers on GitHub (`domain/github-setup.cjs`), each made through the code host's driver — the landing driver serving the state's remote, whatever the project's landing setting — and only on yes. `github-rule` answers the offer of a rule on `leeway-state` refusing its deletion and any push that is not a fast-forward, for anything run outside Claude Code: offered wherever the code host's driver can set it. `github-squash` answers the offer of the squash default — a squash merge's commit made from the pull request's title and description, so each task lands under its own subject and id — offered only where the project also lands through that driver, since landing refuses a stack merge until it is set, and made on every repository the project lands in there: its own, and each nested in it whose own remote the driver serves, each left as it is where it is set already. `yes` makes the setting, `no` makes nothing; either way what happened is recorded on the project manifest — `github_rule`, `github_squash` — as `{"status": "set"|"declined"|"failed", "reason"?}`, `failed` with why, and committed confined to it on the state branch, so the offer is never made again, to this clone or another. A setting the driver could not make is a warning naming the repository and where to make it by hand, and setup carries on. It refuses any answer but `yes` or `no`, a `yes` where the offer is not made, and an offer already answered — but a `yes` to the squash default, which is taken again, setting it on any repository nested since. Response `{"ok": true, "github_rule"|"github_squash": "set"|"declined"|"failed", "reason"?, "committed", "note"?, "warnings"?}`, its commit `chore: {the offer} {status}`.

```bash
engine github-rule <yes|no>      # the answer to setup's offer of the rule on leeway-state
engine github-squash <yes|no>    # the answer to setup's offer of the squash default
```

**`manifest`** — the field surface: read and write work-unit manifest fields by dot-path. Thin dispatch in `engine.cjs`, logic in `domain/fields.cjs`, schema validation and locking from the kernel modules.

**Dot-path addressing.** Every command takes `command <dotpath> [field] [value]`. The path joins work unit, phase, and topic with dots; segment count determines the access level:

| Segments | Level | Path | Field | Resolves to |
|----------|-------|------|-------|-------------|
| 1 | Work unit | `my-epic` | `work_type` | `work_type` |
| 2 | Phase | `my-epic.discovery` | `dismissed` | `phases.discovery.dismissed` |
| 3 | Topic | `my-epic.discussion.auth-flow` | `status` | `phases.discussion.items.auth-flow.status` |

Topic-level access routes through items (`phases.{phase}.items.{topic}.{field}`); the field argument may itself be a dot-path (`sources.auth-api.status`). The wildcard topic `*` collects from every item in a phase (`get`/`exists` only; `get` returns a JSON array of `{topic, value}` objects). The reserved prefix `project` routes to the project manifest (`.leeway/manifest.json`) with the field embedded in the dot-path — `get project.defaults.tmux_labels`, `set project.defaults.tmux_labels true`; a `set`, `push`, `pull` or `delete` of `project.project_id` is refused, the engine minting it as it creates the state and it never changing — which is why `project` is a reserved work-unit name, alongside `roadmap` (the product layer's identity), `product` (the product's conversation) and `none` (the literal a skill takes where an argument names nothing — see `handoff`); dots and phase names are also barred, enforced at `workunit create`.

**Output contract, deliberately split.** Reads print bare stdout — they are prose substitution surfaces: scalars raw (no quotes), subtrees as two-space-indented JSON, `exists` printing `true`/`false` (always exit 0), `key-of` the bare key, `list` a JSON array of manifests, missing `get` paths empty stdout exit 0. Read errors keep `Error: …` on stderr with exit 1 (real error) or exit 2 (expected miss — `key-of` on a missing work unit, or a `key-of` value not found; `get` misses are the empty-stdout exit 0 above, never exit 2). Mutations answer with the engine's one-line JSON response and fail as `{"ok": false, "error": …}` stderr exit 1 like every other verb. `apply <work-unit> --file <ops.json>` is the batch form of set/delete: ops `{"op": "set", "path": <dotpath>, "fields": {…}}` / `{"op": "delete", "path": <dotpath>, "field": …}`, values native JSON (`null` is `null`, never `'~'`), every op validated with the entry named before anything is written, all paths inside the one work unit (one lock, one atomic write — the project manifest is out of scope), a missing delete target failing the whole batch. No git commit — the calling flow's commit covers it.

```bash
engine manifest get    <dotpath> [<field.path>]          # scalar raw / subtree JSON; missing → empty, exit 0
engine manifest set    <dotpath> <field> <value>                 # single field (positional shorthand)
engine manifest set    <dotpath> <field>=<value> [<field>=<value> …]  # uniform batch — never mixed with positional
engine manifest push   <dotpath> <field> <value>         # append, creating the array
engine manifest pull   <dotpath> <field> <value>         # remove first deep-equal match; no-op safe
engine manifest delete <dotpath> <field.path>            # errors when the path is absent
engine manifest apply  <work-unit> --file <ops.json>     # batch set/delete across one work unit — atomic
engine manifest exists <dotpath> [<field.path>]          # true/false, always exit 0
engine manifest list   [--status <s>] [--work-type <t>]  # JSON array of manifests
engine manifest key-of <dotpath> <field.path> <value>    # reverse lookup (e.g. task_map external → internal)
```

`set` batches: additional `<field>=<value>` pairs after the positional pair land under one lock in one write, answered by one response listing every field written (`{"ok": true, "path": …, "set": {…}}`); pairs split on the **first** `=` only, so values may contain `=`. Values parse as JSON first (arrays, objects, numbers, booleans), falling back to string; a bare `~` is null — shell-quote anything zsh would eat (`'[]'`, `'{}'`, `'~'`). Validation is schema-driven (`kernel/manifest-schema.cjs`): `work_type`, work-unit `status`, per-phase item statuses (discovery's empty vocabulary refuses every status write — map items carry no status), and gate modes — a walk's nested `gate_mode`, under its staging row, takes `gated`/`auto`, and any other `*_gate_mode` field is refused as no gate the schema knows. A refused value fails a whole batch with nothing written. A `status` write onto a phase item that is `cancelled`, `postponed` or `promoted` is refused whatever the value, and so is a `delete` of the item or of any field of it — `{phase} item "{topic}" is cancelled — reactivate it instead (engine topic reactivate {wu} discovery {topic})`, the reactivate addressed by its topic for a discovery unit's item and naming the verb alone for any other, `{phase} item "{topic}" is postponed — pull it forward from the roadmap instead`, or `{phase} item "{topic}" is promoted (to "{unit}") — promotion is terminal; continue it from the cross-cutting work unit` — the same refusal every transition makes, so the field surface is never the permissive path around them (the item's other fields stay writable). Dot-paths with empty segments are refused outright (an unset shell variable would alias the project manifest), and work-unit-level mutations (`set`, `push`, `pull`, `apply` set-ops) refuse a field whose first segment names a phase — a typo'd dot-path would build a shadow tree beside `phases.*` that no read joins; reads and `delete` stay free so a stray tree can be inspected and repaired. Mutation responses: `set` echoes the fields written; `push` carries `pushed` and the new `length`; `pull` carries `removed` (`false` on any no-op) and `length`; `delete` carries `deleted: true`.

**`knowledge`** — the knowledge base's one door: a local store of `.leeway/`'s knowledge, searched by meaning and by word: a quick-fix's short record after the work, the gap-analysis cache indexed when topic-discovery rewrites it, and the ledger's records, rendered as copies under `.leeway/.knowledge/records/` — a topic's at `{unit}/{topic}.md`, a unit's own level at `{unit}.md`, phase `record`, and none for the product's place — again at every ledger write and every boot, and indexed (**`ledger`**). Chunks are the text itself at full fidelity, never summaries, each carrying its provenance — work unit, phase, topic, and the source document's date. It surfaces prior context that would otherwise be lost across work units or forgotten within one: a discussion that rejected an approach, an investigation that ruled out a cause. The knowledge directory (`.leeway/.knowledge/` — the store, its metadata, and the knowledge `config.json` setup writes, beside them the store's writer lock (`.lock`) and the vector fill's claim (`.fill`), git-ignored with them) sits in the one state every checkout and worktree of the project shares, and is never committed: the store is derived from the committed artifacts, each clone sets up and builds its own, and a worktree reads the one its main checkout's state holds. Mechanism lives in `kernel/knowledge/` (the store's file format, word search and its stemmer, ranking, chunking, excerpts and outlines, the embedding providers, the config), the workflow side in `domain/knowledge/` (artifact identity and discovery against the manifests, indexing and retirement, the vector fill, query, status, setup; `commands.cjs` is the door), and the per-phase chunking configs in `content/knowledge/chunking/`. The transactions reach the same modules in process — their knowledge-base syncs never go through the door.

**What is indexed**, with the confidence each phase carries:

- `record` (high — a topic's ledger record or a unit's own, as its rendered copy: what was asked and settled, never the log the conversation kept)
- `summary` (high — a quick-fix's short record after the work, `{wu}/summary.md`, each fix's record a chunk of its own)
- `analysis` (low — the gap-analysis cache, a meta-summary derived from low-confidence material)

The spec is never indexed: the records it is drawn from are indexed in its place. Nor is a seed, an asset, an inbox item or the product record's rendered file (`.leeway/.knowledge/product.md`) ever indexed (22.6.12): an asset is reached through the entry that links it, its path in that entry's record. The build and the review are never indexed: they describe execution, not knowledge, and searching them would surface task IDs and code fragments, not insight. A unit's operational `.state/` files are excluded too — only the gap-analysis cache filename is accepted from `.state/`. No log is indexed — a topic's `log.md`, a unit's discovery `session-NNN.md` or the product's in `.leeway/.product/sessions/` — nor any other file under `.leeway/.product/`: what a conversation settles reaches the store as its ledger record.

**Answers in text, not JSON.** Every verb acts on the project the call's directory sits in and prints what it did. The verbs that talk to the embedding provider — the bulk `index`, `query`, `fill`, `setup`, and `rebuild` — wait on it; every other verb answers at once. `--help`, `-h` or `help` writes the usage to stdout, exit 0; a call naming no verb writes it to stderr, exit 1. A failure is said on stderr, exit 1 — `Error: …` wherever the verb does not word it itself. `check` is branched on by its output — it always exits `0`. `query` is branched on first by its exit code, non-zero a failure — a usage error (no term, a blank term, a malformed `--boost`), a knowledge config it cannot use (one that cannot be read, or a provider that cannot be built), or a store that cannot be read or whose metadata is missing — and on `0` by its output.

```bash
engine knowledge query "<term>" ["<term>" …] [--work-unit <wu>] [--work-type <t>] [--phase <p>] [--topic <t>] [--boost:<field> <value> …] [--limit <n>] [--explain]  # in a conversation, kept as its last query in its folder, `knowledge-query.json` — terms, filters, then what it printed or why it failed — what `brief plain-search` draws; no lock
engine knowledge check                                   # ready | buildable | not-ready
engine knowledge index <path/to/artifact.md>             # one artifact — its keyword side at once, its vectors filled in the background
engine knowledge index [--work-unit <wu>]                # the bulk index — the store brought in line with the files, its vectors filled while it waits
engine knowledge fill                                    # embed the chunks awaiting vectors — what an index launches in the background
engine knowledge remove --work-unit <wu> [--phase <p> [--topic <t>]] [--dry-run]
engine knowledge status                                  # full health report — for people, never automation
engine knowledge compact [--dry-run]
engine knowledge rebuild                                 # human-only — asks for `rebuild` typed back
engine knowledge setup [--from-system | --keyword-only | --provider <id> … | --key-only [--provider <id>]]   # no flag: the interactive wizard, human-only
```

`query` searches the store. Several positional terms run as separate searches in one invocation — one store load — merged, deduplicated by chunk ID (highest score wins), then cut to `--limit`. Flags:

| Flag | Behaviour |
|------|-----------|
| `--work-unit <wu>` | Filter to one or more work units. Comma-separated list accepted. Hard filter — non-matching chunks excluded |
| `--work-type <type>` | Filter to a work type. Comma-separated list accepted (e.g., `--work-type bugfix` or `--work-type epic,feature`). Hard filter |
| `--phase <phase>` | Filter to one or more phases. Same comma-separated syntax. Hard filter |
| `--topic <topic>` | Filter to one or more topics. Same comma-separated syntax. Hard filter |
| `--boost:<field> <value>` | **Re-ranking hint, NOT a filter.** Boosts chunks where `<field>` equals `<value>` by `+0.1` per match, additive — keyword-only, that only breaks near-ties. Repeatable. Valid fields: `work-unit`, `work-type`, `phase`, `topic`, `confidence` |
| `--limit <n>` | Cap result count after merge + re-rank. Default 10 |
| `--explain` | Print beneath each result how it ranked — for diagnosing retrieval, never needed to use the results |

**Search modes**, settled per query. **Hybrid** — the store was built with the provider, model and dimensions the config names: every term embedded in one request, keyword and vector searches blended, results re-ranked by any `--boost:<field>` directives, plus an always-on confidence-tier boost and a progress-based decay that down-ranks units the project has moved past. **Keyword-only** — full-text search alone. Still useful: semantic expansion is lost, but a word still finds its other forms (`limit` finds `limiting`), and the commonest words (`the`, `of`, `is`) match nothing. A supported degraded mode, not a broken state. The output opens with a note naming why, and the fix:

- no provider configured — `[keyword-only mode — configure embedding provider for semantic search]`;
- a provider configured over a keyword-only store — the next start embeds the store, no rebuild;
- whatever keeps the query from a vector the store can compare — a key that does not resolve, a provider down or unreachable, a rate limit that outlasts the query's waiting, an account out of quota, a config that dropped its provider, a store built with another provider, model or dimensions (fixed by `knowledge rebuild`).

A query never fails for want of a vector: whatever its note says, it exits `0` with its results. Nor does it stall on the provider: each request gets 5 seconds to answer and a second try, and a rate limit only the waiting a query is allowed (see `index`), before the query runs keyword-only.

**Output.** A `[N results]` header — the merged, deduplicated, re-ranked count after `--limit`; `[0 results]` with nothing beneath it when nothing matched — then per result a provenance line, `[phase | work_unit/topic | confidence | YYYY-MM-DD]` (the source document's date, its mtime); the headings enclosing its excerpt, title first, joined by ` › `; the excerpt; and a `Source:` line naming the source artifact at the chunk's lines in it (`path:L3-39`, one line `path:L9`, counted from the file's first line, frontmatter included), a blank line between results. The excerpt is the passage of the chunk, at most 1000 characters, whose lines share the most of the query's words — the words of the framing the result ranked on, as the search tokenizes them, rarer words weighing more — widened by the lines around it; heading lines are never part of it, a chunk with no line sharing a word shows its opening lines, and a line over the budget alone is cut at word boundaries around its matching stretch, `…` marking each cut. The headings and the range are read from the file as it stands: where the file no longer holds the chunk's text (edited since its last index, or gone), the headings line is absent and `Source:` names the bare path; a document with no heading enclosing the excerpt has no headings line either. Notes sit above the header, one line each: why the query ran keyword-only (see Search modes), and, over a store with chunks awaiting vectors, `[N chunks await vectors — searched by keyword alone; each start retries them]`, followed, where the last vector fill fell short, by `[the last vector fill fell short — …]` naming the first file it failed and why; then a `[knowledge config — …]` line for each config setting the load ignored (see `setup`). `--explain` adds, beneath each source line, a `Framing N:` line per term — its keyword score and vector similarity, each raw → over its search's best (`absent` where that search missed the chunk), and their blend (keyword-only: the raw keyword score). A framing whose hits, cut to twice the limit, lack the chunk reads `not in its top 20` at the default limit, whether or not the term matched it. Then `Score:` names the framing whose score the chunk kept and works it through decay, boosts and confidence tier to the final value.

`check` is the readiness probe. It always exits `0` (unless the filesystem itself is unreadable) and prints one state: `ready` — the knowledge base is initialised and the store is loadable; `buildable` — this clone is set up (its `.leeway/.knowledge/config.json`) but has no store, and this machine's config says how to build one: a provider whose key resolves, or keyword-only chosen outright — a system config holding knowledge settings that name no provider, or a project config unsetting the provider. No system config, an invalid one, or a provider whose key cannot be resolved is never read as a keyword-only choice; `not-ready` — missing directory, missing config, unloadable store, or a missing store this machine cannot build. `boot` runs it at every start: `buildable` has boot build the store; `not-ready` routes into leeway's knowledge gate, which drives `setup` through its non-interactive forms.

`index` writes to the store, in two forms. **With a file** — what a skill runs over a document it writes outside a transaction; the transactions index in process — it writes the file's chunks searchable by keyword and returns at once, re-indexing replacing the file's existing chunks (idempotent); it then launches the vector fill (see `fill`) when a provider that can embed is configured and chunks await vectors. The path, absolute or from the working folder, must be in the state (`Not in the workflows state: {path}` otherwise) and name an indexed artifact so identity can be derived — an identity named from the state root, so it never moves when the checkout does. For a quick-fix's short record, the path is `.leeway/{work_unit}/summary.md`. For the gap-analysis cache, the path is `.leeway/{work_unit}/.state/discovery-gap-analysis.md`; the phase is `analysis` and the topic is `gap-analysis`. A seed, an asset, an inbox item, a session log or any file under `.leeway/.product/` has no identity and is refused. Output: `Indexed N chunks from {file}`. A file that fails to index exits non-zero, saying the next start retries it.

**Without a file** — the bulk index — it brings the store in line with the files and fills the vectors it is owed while it waits. A clone with no store gets one, in the mode this machine's config names — empty when there is nothing to index, its metadata replacing any left without a store. Every chunk records the sha256 of the content it was indexed from and the version of the chunker that cut it; the bulk index compares those against each discovered artifact (the record copies, a quick-fix's short record, gap-analysis caches) and indexes the ones with no chunks (`new`) or with chunks from other content or another chunker version (`changed` — a chunk with no recorded hash or chunker version counts as changed, and a re-cut that leaves a text as it was keeps its vector; a file with a chunk awaiting its vector is cut again, and counts as changed when its chunk texts differ from the stored ones); the rest are `unchanged`. It skips the artifacts `compact` prunes, so it never re-embeds them. It then removes the chunks of identities positively known to be gone, deciding from the manifests as they stand when it writes: a source file inside the project no longer on disk, and a work unit absent from a non-empty registry or cancelled. A work unit whose manifest cannot be read keeps its chunks. `--work-unit <wu>` confines both halves to one unit. Output is a line per file acted on, then `N new, N changed, N removed, N unchanged.` (`, N failed` when any file failed to index, `, N chunks awaiting vectors` when any chunk's vectors failed). Setup and `rebuild` end with it; `boot` runs its keyword half at every start and hands the vectors to the fill.

**Keywords first, then vectors.** Either form writes each file's chunks searchable by keyword — carrying the vector of any text the store already holds — before a vector is embedded, about 100 texts to a request, each batch saved as it lands. The bulk index embeds every chunk awaiting a vector in its scope — a pruned one excepted — so a chunk whose embed failed gets its vector at the next start, bar one the endpoint refused. A keyword-only store takes a provider configured over it at its next index, and every chunk its vector — no rebuild. Over a store built with a provider whose key does not resolve, the chunks are written all the same and no fill is launched; the bulk index exits non-zero naming the key's fix (`Cannot embed: …`) even with no chunk awaiting a vector. A store built with one provider, model or dimensions refuses another, as it refuses a config that names no provider: that takes a `knowledge rebuild`.

A rate-limited request (HTTP 429) waits the time the provider names — capped at a minute, growing waits when it names none — and is retried alone, up to five times. An index or fill waits at most 60 seconds in all, a query 5: a request whose wait does not fit what remains fails at once, as does one from an account out of quota. Other transient failures of a request to the provider (network, server error) are retried with exponential backoff — three tries in all, two for a query; nothing but a provider request is retried. A rejected request (HTTP 400/413/422), a bad key, or a provider configuration the model contradicts fails at once. A file whose vectors fail stays searchable by keyword: the bulk index names it on stderr (`Failed to embed <file>: …`) — its vectors coming at the next start, or, where the endpoint refused a chunk of it, that chunk going without one — and exits non-zero. When the endpoint refuses an input in a batch, the batch is embedded file by file, and a refused file text by text, so the refused text alone goes without its vector; any other failure stops the embedding, the files left awaiting named with it. The bulk index attempts every file, names each failure on stderr, and exits non-zero at the end. A manifest the bulk index cannot read aborts it before anything is removed. Either form exits non-zero if the file doesn't exist, the path can't be parsed, or the path names a non-markdown import — and when there is no store to write and this machine's config does not say how to create one (the rule `buildable` states): a provider whose key cannot be resolved is never stood in for by a keyword-only store, and no configuration at all is never read as a keyword-only choice.

`fill` embeds every chunk awaiting a vector a provider can give — bar those `compact` prunes — batch by batch, saving each as it lands (the rules above). A single-file index, a transaction's knowledge-base sync, and `boot` each launch it detached when a provider that can embed is configured and chunks await vectors — never for a keyword-only store, nor for a provider whose key does not resolve — so none of them waits on embedding and vectors land seconds after a phase closes. One fill works at a time: a second finds the first's claim (`.leeway/.knowledge/.fill`) and ends at once. It is silent unless a chunk went without its vector, when it names each file on stderr and exits non-zero. A fill that falls short records why in the store's metadata (`fill_failure` — the first file it failed, why, and how many more), said in the next query's note, in `status`, and in boot's warnings; a fill that lands everything clears it, and the next start retries what awaits.

`remove` removes the chunks matching its filter: `--work-unit <wu>` alone every chunk of the unit, `--phase <p>` narrowing to one phase, `--topic <t>` (which requires `--phase`) to one topic. A work unit the registry does not hold is refused unless the store still holds its chunks — then removed as an orphan cleanup. `--dry-run` counts what the filter matches without touching the store. Output: `Removed N chunks for {scope}`. No skill runs it: the transactions that retire a unit's material — a work unit's cancel, an absorb — remove its chunks in process. Exits non-zero on usage errors and when the removal fails, saying the next start removes them.

`status` is the human-readable health report: chunk counts by work unit, phase, and work type; last-indexed timestamp; provider info; the mode a query runs in — keyword-only naming why and the fix, in its note's words; the chunks awaiting vectors in a store built with a provider, and why the last vector fill fell short; a knowledge config that cannot load, named, and each config setting the load ignored (`WARNING: …`); and what the next bulk `index` does — artifacts not yet indexed, artifacts changed since they were indexed, artifacts pruned below the decay floor (never counted as unindexed or changed), and indexed identities it retires, each with its reason. Not used in skill automation — intended for debugging and user inspection.

`rebuild` is destructive: it deletes the existing index and re-indexes everything currently discoverable — the record copies, each quick-fix's short record, and any present gap-analysis caches (`.state/discovery-gap-analysis.md`). It asks the user to type `rebuild` literally to confirm — read from the terminal, or from the first line of stdin where there is none — refusing before the prompt, the store untouched, where this machine's config does not say how to create a store (the rule `buildable` states) — and sets the old store aside until the new one stands, putting it back if the index throws. **Human-only** — no skill runs it. Non-deterministic: rebuilt chunks won't match the originals (embedding variance, edited artifacts).

`compact [--dry-run]` is the storage backstop: it removes a work unit's chunks once their retrievability `R` has decayed below `decay_prune_below` — once enough later work has completed that they're effectively unreachable in query ranking. Decay is progress-based (how much work completed after the unit, weighted by work type), not wall-clock; `false` disables it. `--dry-run` previews without deleting. Skills call neither `compact` nor `rebuild`: `boot` compacts at every start, and `rebuild` is the user's.

`setup` initialises the knowledge base: the system config (`~/.config/leeway/config.json`), this clone's init (`.leeway/.knowledge/` — never committed, so every copy of the project sets up once), and an initial bulk index of every indexed artifact. An artifact that fails to index, or a chunk that fails to embed, does not fail setup: the output names how many, and the next start retries them. Config resolves defaults ← system ← project; `null` unsets a key. Setup writes provider identity only — the provider settings `provider`, `model`, `dimensions` and `base_url`, replaced as a set, every other key of the file's `knowledge` object kept as written. The tuning keys `similarity_threshold` (the vector leg's cosine floor, 0.3; a number from 0 to 1), `decay_prune_below` (0.05; `false` disables, else a number from 0 to 1), `decay_base_stability` (5; a number above 0) and `decay_weights` (per work type, numbers of 0 or more) are honoured as overrides in either file and never written. A key the knowledge base does not know, or a tuning value outside its range, is ignored — the level beneath it, or the default, applies — and named as `<key> in <file> is ignored: <why>` — in the query's notes and in `status` over a store, and in boot's warnings wherever boot brings a store in line or builds one; the provider settings are used as written, a wrong one failing where it is used. **The API key never passes through a flag, a chat, or stdout.** There is deliberately no `--key` flag — any setup invocation carrying one is refused (argv lands in shell history and process listings). Keys resolve from the provider env var (`$OPENAI_API_KEY` — wins) or `~/.config/leeway/credentials.json` (mode 0600, written by `--key-only` or the wizard). Setup output names active settings only (provider · model), never key material. The non-interactive forms, which skills run:

```bash
engine knowledge setup --from-system
engine knowledge setup --keyword-only
engine knowledge setup --provider openai --model <m> [--dimensions <d>]
engine knowledge setup --provider openai-compatible --base-url <u> --model <m> --dimensions <d>
engine knowledge setup --key-only [--provider <id>]
```

- **`--from-system`** — reuse the existing system config: resolve the key (env → credentials; providerless configs need none), validate provider configs with one test embed, create the project store, bulk-index the existing artifacts, print the active-settings summary. Refuses clearly when the system config is missing/invalid or the openai key is unresolvable (the message names the env var and the `--key-only` remedy).
- **`--keyword-only`** — project-level keyword-only init: project config, empty store, provider-null metadata. Never touches the system config — the project config always pins `provider: null`, so this clone runs keyword-only whatever the machine's system config names, and when its store goes missing it is `buildable` keyword-only. A system config it cannot read is refused before anything is written. Idempotent; partial states fill in; a store without metadata refuses toward `knowledge rebuild`.
- **`--provider ...`** — write the system config from flags (validation embed first — a broken provider never lands on disk), then proceed as `--from-system`. A change of provider keeps the file's tuning overrides and every top-level key another subsystem keeps there. `openai` requires a resolvable key; `openai-compatible` allows keyless endpoints and requires `--dimensions` (must match the model's native output).
- **`--key-only`** — the terminal detour: a masked readline prompt for the key alone (TTY-required; non-TTY aborts), written to `credentials.json` (mode 0600), then exit. `--provider` selects which provider the key is stored under (default `openai`).

`setup` with no flags runs the guided wizard — **human-only**, prompting throughout at the terminal; a run without one aborts with `engine knowledge setup requires an interactive terminal`. Safe to re-run: per-step prompts detect existing state and offer skip or reconfigure; the bulk index at the end only indexes what is new or changed. The provider menu offers `openai` (cloud, requires an API key), `openai-compatible` (any local/self-hosted OpenAI-compatible `/v1/embeddings` endpoint — LM Studio, Ollama, vLLM, LiteLLM), or `skip` (keyword-only). For `openai-compatible`, the wizard collects `base_url` (required), `model`, and `dimensions`; the API key is **optional** (press Enter to omit for open servers) and is stored only in `credentials.json` — there is no env-var override. `base_url` is consumed only under the `openai-compatible` provider and ignored under `openai`. Configured dimensions must match the local model's native output; the validation embed fails loudly on a mismatch.

**Exit codes.** `0` — success, `check` reporting any state, or a `query` run keyword-only for want of a vector. Non-zero — a usage error, a knowledge config that cannot be read or names a provider that cannot be built, file not found, unparseable path, lock contention exceeded, an index refusing a provider the store was not built with, an index or remove that failed (the bulk index and `fill` also when any chunk's vectors failed, and the bulk index over a store whose provider key does not resolve), or a `query` over a store that cannot be read or whose metadata is missing. `query` with zero results exits `0` and prints a `[0 results]` header.

**`workunit`** — work-unit lifecycle. `create` is the work-type commit — discovery's durability boundary as one transaction. It creates the manifest (identity fields, `status: in-progress`, empty phases, project-manifest registration; a name a work unit already holds refuses in the validate phase, nothing moved — the calling flow derives another), moves each `--seed` — a live inbox path, its folder deriving the `inbox:{idea|bug|quickfix}` source tag — into `seeds/`, installs the `--session-log-file` content verbatim as `discovery/session-001.md` (the content is model-authored — the engine never writes prose), sets `phases.discovery.active_session = "001"` for an epic, and commits scoped to the work unit and the project manifest, plus `.leeway/.inbox` when seeds moved out of it (`discovery({wu}): create work unit ({work_type})` — engine-owned message). Landing filenames are normalised at the last dot: the stem lowercased with whitespace/punctuation runs collapsed to `-` and trimmed, a markdown-ish extension (`.md`, `.markdown`, `.txt`, `.text`) or none yielding `.md`, any other extension kept and lowercased — a seed whose stem normalises away falling back to `seed.md` — and collisions suffix the stem `-2`, `-3` against both the destination directory and the batch. Every seed gets a `seeds[]` manifest entry with a fresh ISO-8601 UTC timestamp, and is never indexed. Validation completes before any mutation. Response: `{"ok": true, "work_unit": …, "work_type": …, "seeds": [{"path": …, "source": …}…], "session_log": …, "committed": "<short-sha>", "warnings": []}`.

`cancel` and `reactivate` are the lifecycle transactions, and completion the third — run in process at finish's yes (**`finish`**), with no verb of its own — each one transaction: manifest write, knowledge-base sync (warn-don't-block), scoped commit (`.leeway/{wu}`). Completion and `cancel` also purge the unit's scratch cache (`.leeway/.cache/{wu}/` — rebuildable per-phase scratch; reactivation regenerates on demand): disk-only, except pre-gitignore tracked residue whose deletions ride the transaction commit. Completion sets `status: completed` and stamps `completed_at` (today, UTC — same form as `created`), its commit message finish's (`finish({wu}): completed`). No knowledge-base action — completed units retain their chunks — except when completing a cancelled unit with a finished pipeline directly (the one cancelled state completion accepts): cancellation removed the unit's chunks, so that path re-indexes them (warn-don't-block). `cancel` sets `status: cancelled` and, under the same lock, moves each of its topics' waiting mail to the inbox as ideas — every topic's but a postponed one's, whose mail waits for its return — each once, as `topic remove` moves it (`inbox: [paths]` on the response, absolute, the ideas riding the commit); it removes the unit's chunks from the knowledge base, in process; commit message engine-owned: `workflow({wu}): mark as cancelled`. It also runs the roadmap's cancel-revert hop, whole-unit form: every roadmap item joined to the unit returns to waiting (`roadmap_reverted: [names]` on the response, project manifest riding the commit — see `roadmap`); reactivate never re-joins, and the mail stays in the inbox. `reactivate` restores `status: in-progress` from completed **or** cancelled — a finished pipeline (derived next phase `done`) is no bar — clears a stale `completed_at`, and — only from cancelled, whose removal emptied the index — re-indexes what cancellation removed (its record copies, a quick-fix's short record, and on-disk analysis caches); commit message engine-owned: `workflow({wu}): reactivate work unit`. Illegal transitions refuse loudly: completion and cancel refuse an already-completed and an already-cancelled unit respectively and route the opposite closed state through reactivate (completion alone also accepts the cancelled finished-pipeline state); reactivate refuses an in-progress unit. Statuses are validated against the shared schema's work-unit vocabulary — the engine is never the permissive path. Responses: `{"ok": true, "work_unit": …, "status": …, "committed": "<short-sha>", "warnings": []}` plus `completed_at` (completion) or `previous_status` (reactivate); `committed: null` plus a note when nothing was staged.

`pivot` converts an in-progress feature to an epic as one transaction: flips `work_type: epic` in the work-unit manifest **and** the project manifest's registration (upserted when a legacy unit predates registration), registers the feature's single topic (topic name = work unit name) on the discovery map with backfill semantics (summary and description left for the next epic entry's summary-backfill), first in the map's order, and records the map approved at the pivot (`phases.discovery.approved_at`, 18.3.5), in the same save, re-indexes the whole unit via the same walk reactivate uses — chunk metadata carries `work_type`, which is why pivot re-indexes — and commits both manifests (`workflow({wu}): pivot to epic`, engine-owned). Refuses a non-feature, a unit that isn't in-progress, one past its spec (`{wu} has its spec — it carries on as work of its own`, **`enter`**), and a topic already on (or dismissed from) the map — all before anything is touched. Response: `{"ok": true, "work_unit": …, "work_type": "epic", "committed": "<short-sha>", "warnings": []}`.

`absorb` merges a feature into an in-progress epic as a new topic, then deletes the feature — one transaction owning the mechanical tail; the judgment (choosing the feature, the epic, and the topic name with the user) stays in the calling prose, and the verb takes the decided inputs. Validation completes before any mutation — feature exists and is a feature with a discussion (item **and** file), before its spec (refused as `pivot` refuses one past it); every discussion and experiment item is named after the feature (a foreign-named item refuses loudly — the feature directory is deleted afterwards, so a silent skip would lose it); epic exists, is an epic, is in-progress, before its spec (`{epic} has its spec, so its map is closed — absorb the feature into an epic still before its spec, or keep it as work of its own`); the topic name is one the map takes, a lowercase slug of letters, digits and hyphens, and free in the epic (a map item, or the title another goes by, the dismissed list, a discussion item, an experiment item, the topic's folder on disk); and, where the feature holds ledger lines, the conversation names its session — so a refusal leaves both work units byte-identical: no crash window between the feature's deletion and the commit. It moves the feature's topic folder whole, `{feature}/topics/{feature}/` renamed to the epic's `topics/{topic}/` — its log, its brief, its mailbox and its experiments' records, every file keeping its name — at the topic name, so the map row, the artifacts, and every experiment join share one name; the experiment series when the feature ran one (`phases.experiment.items.{topic}` a whole copy of the item — derived status and every record with its slug, status, verdict, or reason); its seeds and assets — each file or folder at the top of `assets/` but a dot-named one, tooling state that goes with the feature; and its spikes, each `spikes/{NNN}-{slug}/` keeping its number, a slug one of the epic's spikes holds already suffixed `-2`, `-3` so no two share one, and its twin under the cache moved with it (filename collisions suffix `-2`, `-3` — the shared landing dedupe — with manifest entries carrying their **original** timestamps and seed provenance; a file or spike the dedupe renamed has its `../seeds/{old}`, `../assets/{old}` or `../spikes/{old}` links — the anchored relative form a document writes, any depth of `../`, the name ending where no name character follows it or a dot followed by none, a sentence's full stop — rewritten to the new name in each markdown file of the topic folder this absorb moved — the one content substitution the domain ring makes, bounded to a path its own rename broke, exact and idempotent — and an asset or a spike renamed is reported under `renamed_assets` or `renamed_spikes`); carries each phase item whole onto the epic — the dismissed grounds, a live `awaiting_experiments` (the series moving topic-keyed beside it so the completion refusal and release edges keep holding) — never a field at a time; registers the map item with backfill semantics (no summary or description), in the epic's one save with the items; carries the feature's ledger lines to the epic topic (5.8.18) — one `rehomed` line at `{epic}.discussion.{topic}`, appended once the epic's manifest holds the topic, listing by id every line made at a place of the feature, its unit's level, its discussion and its experiments, and as `files` each file those lines cite or link that the absorb moved or renamed, `[from, to]`, which every read takes from then on as made at the epic topic, naming each such file where it went (**`ledger`**), none where the feature holds no line — so a unit made later under the feature's name holds none of them, and a line another clone writes at the feature after the absorb is no line it lists; removes the feature's knowledge-base chunks (warn-don't-block); adds the calls the feature's last-looked mark holds to the epic's, so a call seen in the feature stays seen at the epic topic; deletes the feature directory, its project-manifest registration, its mark and its scratch cache (`.leeway/.cache/{feature}/`); and lands ONE commit staging `.leeway/{feature}`, `.leeway/{epic}`, and `.leeway/manifest.json`, and the ledger where it carried lines (`workflow({feature}): absorb into {epic}`, engine-owned); the record copies follow as after any command that changes what is live, the feature's removed and the epic topic's rendered and indexed. Roadmap joins follow the material: every item pulled into the feature is re-aimed at the epic — an idea at `{epic, topic}`, a bug or a quick-fix at `{epic}` alone, riding with the unit as material, never a topic (`roadmap_reaimed: [names]` rides the response) — the un-pull is cancel's move, never absorb's — and so do sources: an item source under the feature, waiting or pulled, is rewritten to the path this absorb moved its file to (`roadmap_sources_rewritten: [{"item": …, "from": …, "to": …}…]`) or dropped where the feature's deletion took the file, the item's `sources` going with the last of them (`roadmap_sources_dropped: [{"item": …, "source": …}…]`); each roadmap field rides only when non-empty. The feature's `discovery/` logs, and anything else outside its topic folder and those four homes, are not moved — they are deleted with the feature directory; git history is provenance. Response: `{"ok": true, "feature": …, "epic": …, "topic": …, "discussion": {"path": …, "status": …}, "assets": […], "renamed_assets": [{"from": …, "to": …}…], "spikes": […], "renamed_spikes": [{"from": …, "to": …}…], "seeds": […], "committed": "<short-sha>", "warnings": []}` (each renamed list empty when nothing collided), plus `experiment: {"path": …, "status": …, "experiments": [ids]}` when a series moved and `mail_moved: {"path": …, "count": N}` (the topic's mailbox in the epic, absolute) when messages waited.

`move` moves a quick-fix to a feature or a bugfix, or a bugfix to a feature, from inside the unit's own session (19.5.9, 19.4.22; never from manage, 18.3.4), through start this work's screen and its yes (**`start-this-work`**). **`move <work-unit> --to feature|bugfix --file <payload.json>`** checks the unit, then stages Claude's payload, exactly `{"description", "reason", "brief"}` — the description for the new type, the one-line reason for it, and the one topic's brief, a draft in the cache drawn from the change or the investigation — where start this work stages a start, `{"type", "name", "description", "reason", "brief", "moves": {"from", "branches", "aside"}}`, and answers `{"ok": true, "work_unit", "from", "to", "warnings"?}` and then start this work's screen drawn from it: the unit's title and new type, the description, `↳` the reason and, for a quick-fix whose branch in any repository holds commits past the branch it lands on, one line naming each name it is kept under, `⚑ Its commits stay unlanded on moved/fix/{wu}[ in {repo}], …`, then, for each fix's work its park set aside, which the move keeps, a line for each repository holding it, `⚑ Task {N}.{n}'s work, set aside when a question parked it, is kept in {the project's repository|{repo}} at {ref} — see it with {see}; bring it into the checkout with {recover}, settling any conflict`, then, where the fix holding the checkout has work not committed, which the yes sets aside, a line for each repository holding it, `⚑ Task {N}.{n}'s work not committed — {files} — is set aside as the quick-fix moves and kept in {the project's repository|{repo}} at {ref} — see it then with {see}; bring it into the checkout with {recover}, settling any conflict` (`aside`, its words as staged); the MENU's lead says `Your yes makes the {from} a {type}.` and where the work opens next, and `y/yes` → `Move it — the {from} becomes a {type}` runs `start-this-work yes`. Nothing moves before the yes. It refuses, writing nothing: a unit not in progress (`{wu} is {status} — only work in progress moves`); a unit of a type the reshape retires, in the one line every door gives it (`{wu} is a cross-cutting unit, a type the reshape retires — …`); a type that does not move (`{wu} is an epic, which moves nowhere — a quick-fix moves to a feature or a bugfix, and a bugfix to a feature`, a feature the same) and a move its type does not take (`{wu} is a bugfix, which moves to a feature — not to "{to}"`); a quick-fix the old scoping made (`{wu} was scoped under the old plan, and moves once the reshape's 29.17 migration has cut it`); a unit past its spec (`{wu} has its spec — it carries on as work of its own`); a bugfix but from its investigation in progress (`a bugfix moves to a feature from its investigation, and {wu}'s has concluded — the work carries on in its spec`, `… has not begun`), or while the root cause its log holds has no validation taken in since it was logged, so the feature never starts from a cause its validator would have broken (19.4.7) — `{wu} moves to a feature once its root cause's validation is taken in, and ` then the conclude's own words for it (**`investigation`**), one still running `… — the move waits for its report`; none where no root cause is logged; a quick-fix a task of which — a fix, or a fix task of its review — has landed in any repository it touches, a merge of a pull request by hand among them, as the landing reads it whatever the tracker holds (`{wu}'s fix {N.n} has landed — finish the quick-fix, then start the {to} as work of its own`, the lowest named) — each pull request open and not merged the yes's to close, one the person closed by hand read as none open; and a payload not of that shape, a description or reason empty, or a brief draft missing or empty. **The yes** — `start-this-work yes` over a staged move — checks the move again against the unit as it stands, each refusal above given at the yes, and, where the unit holds ledger lines the move carries, that the conversation names its session (`{wu} holds ledger entries, which the move carries to its topic in this conversation — Claude Code hands every command it runs a session id, and this one has none`). Then each step is one a second yes repeats or finds done: for a quick-fix, it sets aside what the fix holding the checkout has not committed, as a park sets it aside (**`raise`**), at `refs/leeway/aside/{wu}/{task id}` in each repository holding any, its commit saying it was set aside as the quick-fix moved — refused, nothing set aside and nothing moved, where any step of it fails — then takes each checkout back to the branch its repository lands on where it has the quick-fix's branch, `fix/{wu}`, out — refused first, nothing moved, while it still holds changes not committed outside the state, the person's own (`{wu}'s tasks leave the project's checkout, which holds changes not committed — {files} — so it was not taken back to {branch}: commit them on fix/{wu} or set them aside, then say yes again`) — and closes each of its tasks not yet ended, its fixes and its review's fix tasks (`task close`, reason `{wu} moved to a {type}`), as the verb closes it but for its refusal of a fix holding work, which the move lands nothing of — a fix recorded having its record taken out — each close committing as the verb does. It carries every line made in the unit but discovery's — those stay at its own level, read as any unit's — to the topic by one `rehomed` line at `{wu}.{discussion|investigation}.{wu}` listing them (**`ledger`**), so they read as the topic's at the new type's first phase, the input its first opening reads, and no reader of a later phase takes one as its own: a quick-fix's call is no call of the feature's build.
